Asymptotic behavior of the multilevel type error for SDEs driven by a pure jump Lévy process Thanks: This research is supported by Laboratory of Excellence MME-DII, Grant no. ANR11LBX-0023-01 ( http://labex-mme-dii.u-cergy.fr/ ). Ahmed Kebaier benefited from the support of the chair Risques Financiers, Fondation du Risque.
Mohamed BEN ALAYA, Ahmed KEBAIER, Thi Bao Tram NGO

Abstract
. Motivated by the multilevel Monte Carlo method introduced by Giles [ 5 ] , we study the asymptotic behavior of the normalized error process u n , m ​ ( X n − X n ​ m ) u_{n,m}(X^{n}-X^{nm}) where X n X^{n} and X n ​ m X^{nm} are respectively Euler approximations with time steps 1 / n 1/n and 1 / n ​ m 1/nm of a given stochastic differential equation X X driven by a pure jump Lévy process. In this paper, we prove that this normalized multilevel error converges to different non-trivial limiting processes with various sharp rates u n , m u_{n,m} depending on the behavior of the Lévy measure around zero. Our results are consistent with those of Jacod [ 9 ] obtained for the normalized error u n ​ ( X n − X ) u_{n}(X^{n}-X) , as when letting m m tends to infinity, we recover the same limiting processes. For the multilevel error, the proofs of the current paper are challenging since unlike [ 9 ] we need to deal with m m dependent triangular arrays instead of one.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 60J75, 65C30; secondary 60J30, 60F17

1.
Introduction

Suppose that we are in the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T},\mathbb{P})
endowed with the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{s}=\sigma(Y_{u},u\leq s)
, where
Y
is a Lévy process with characteristics
(b,c,F)
with respect to the truncation function
h(x)=x\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\leq 1\}}
, meaning

\mathbb{E}(e^{\texttt{i}uY_{t}})=\exp\Big\{t\big(\texttt{i}ub-\frac{cu^{2}}{2}+\int F(dx)(e^{\texttt{i}ux}-1-\texttt{i}ux\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\leq 1\}}\big)\Big\}.

We consider the Lévy driven stochastic differential equation (SDE)

X_{t}=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}f(X_{s-})dY_{s},\hskip 28.45274ptt\in[0,T],\hskip 2.84544ptT>0

(1.1)

where
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
(a three-times-differentiable function). Without loss of generality, we assume that
T=1
. In what follows, we consider the continuous Euler approximation

\displaystyle dX_{t}^{n}=f(X^{n}_{\eta_{n}(t-)})dY_{t},\hskip 28.45274ptt\in[0,1]

(1.2)

with time step
1/n
, where
\eta_{n}(t)=\frac{[nt]}{n}
.

For the error process
X^{n}-X
, Jacod and Protter
[
10
]
proved that the sharp rate is
\sqrt{n}
when the characteristic triplet corresponds to
(b,c,F)
with
c>0
. Let us precise that a rate is called sharp if the normalized error converges to a non-trivial limiting process. Then, Jacod
[
9
]
established new sharp rates of convergence different from the classical
\sqrt{n}
rate for several cases with a Lévy characteristic triplet
(b,0,F)
and
F(\mathbb{R})=\infty
. Those cases correspond to different behaviors of the Lévy measure
F
around zero. More recently, Wang
[
16
]
extended the results of Jacod
[
9
]
for the case of general Itô semimartingales.

In the current paper, motivated by the multilevel Monte Carlo method introduced by Giles
[
5
]
, we study instead the error between two Euler schemes with different time steps. In particular, we are interested in determining sharp rates
u_{n,m}
for the weak convergence of the error between two consecutive Euler approximations

X^{n}-X^{nm}
and identifying the corresponding limiting processes. Here,
X^{n}
and
X^{nm}
stand for the Euler schemes with respectively time steps
1/n
and
1/mn
that are build on the same Lévy paths. In the literature, several papers studied this multilevel type error. Indeed, when the characteristic triplet of
Y
is
(b,1,0)
, Ben Alaya and Kebaier
[
10
]
proved that

\;\;(Y,\sqrt{\frac{mn}{(m-1)}}(X^{nm}-X^{n}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle stably}}{{\Rightarrow}}(Y,U),\mbox{ as }n\to\infty

with
m\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0,1\}
,

U_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}f^{\prime}(X_{s})U_{s}dY_{s}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{0}^{t}f(X_{s})f^{\prime}(X_{s})dW_{s}

and

W
is a new standard Brownian motion independent of
Y
.
When the characteristic triplet of
Y
is
(b,c,F)
with
c\neq 0
, Dereich and Li
[
4
]
proved a similar result with a sharp rate
\sqrt{n}
under some regularity condition on
F
around zero with an explicit limiting process.

Therefore, to fill the gap in the literature for the analysis of this type of error, we consider
Y
as a Lévy process with characteristics
(b,0,F)
where
F
is an infinite measure. More precisely, for the same cases studied by Jacod
[
9
]
we consider in the current work the multilevel type error between two consecutive Euler approximations defined by

\displaystyle U^{n,m}=X^{n}_{\eta_{nm}}-X^{nm}_{\eta_{nm}}.

(1.3)

For this multilevel type error, we use triangular arrays technics to find the sharp rate of convergence that turns out to be faster than
\sqrt{n}
which is the usual rate when
Y
incorporates a continuous Gaussian part. It is worth noticing that the work of
[
9
]
when studying the error
X^{n}_{\eta_{n}}-X_{\eta_{n}}
needs only to treat one main contributing triangular array.
However, in the current work, the technical challenge we faced when proving the convergence of the correctly normalized multilevel error
(
1.3
)
consists in studying the asymptotic behavior of the joint probability distribution of
m
triangular arrays. These dependent triangular arrays appear naturally when studying the multilevel error obtained between the finer discretization with time step
1/nm
and the coarse one with time step
1/n
. To overcome this problem, we develop new treatments and proofs to handle these
m
dependent terms that contribute in the limit in different ways depending on the assumptions taken on the original Lévy measure
F
around zero. In more details, besides using the “subsequences principle” trick (see e.g. Jacod and Protter
[
11
]
), we use arguments of Sato
[
15
, Ex.12.8-12.10]
that let us avoid complicated calculations of multi-dimensional integrals and rather focus on the pairwise asymptotic behavior of the
m
marginals and we conclude using technical criteria of Kallenberg
[
14
, Theorem 15.14 and Corollary 15.16]
to prove the weak convergence to the limiting process.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, using similar notations, we recall from Jacod
[
9
]
some assumptions and estimates on the Lévy measure and also the semimartingale decomposition. Here, in the spirit of Jacod
[
9
]
, we precise our consideration for five specific cases. In Section
3
, we introduce and prove our main results namely Theorem
3.1
for the tightness, Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
3.4
the functional limit theorems for the couple of normalized errors. Section
4
gives the details of the error analysis to prove our main results with specifying the main and rest terms for each cases and the study of the asymptotic behaviors of the joint distribution of the main terms. The rest terms are treated in appendix
A
. Appendices
B
and
C
are dedicated to recall some technical tools that we use throughout the paper.

2.
General settings and notations

Let
f
denotes a real-valued function
f
satisfying

f\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
and globally Lipschitz.

(
H
f
)

It is well known that assumption
(
Hf
)
guarantees that
(
1.1
)
has an unique non-exploding solution. The crucial factor to find the sharp rates of the multilevel type error
(
1.3
)
is the behavior of the Lévy measure
F
near
0
, which will be expressed through the following functions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\theta_{+}(\beta)

\displaystyle=F((\beta,+\infty)),\hskip 28.45274pt\theta_{-}(\beta)=F((-\infty,-\beta)),

\displaystyle\theta(\beta)

\displaystyle=\theta_{+}(\beta)+\theta_{-}(\beta).

Note that from now on, we denote
C
as a generic constant which may change from line to line. We keep the same framework as in Jacod
[
9
]
and we introduce the four main classes of Lévy measures
F
that we are interested in:

\textrm{We have }\theta(\beta)\leq\frac{C}{\beta^{\alpha}}\textrm{ for all }\beta\in(0,1].

(
H
{}_{1}^{\alpha}
)

\textrm{We have }\beta^{\alpha}\theta_{+}(\beta)\rightarrow\theta_{+}\textrm{ and }\beta^{\alpha}\theta_{-}(\beta)\rightarrow\theta_{-}\textrm{ as }\beta\rightarrow 0\textrm{ for some constants }\theta_{+}\textrm{, }\theta_{-}\geq 0.

(
H
{}_{2}^{\alpha}
)

\textrm{ We set }\theta=\theta_{+}+\theta_{-}>0\textrm{ and }\theta^{\prime}=\theta_{+}-\theta_{-},\textrm{ as }\beta\rightarrow 0\textrm{, }\theta(\beta)\sim\frac{\theta}{\beta^{\alpha}}.

\textrm{The measure }F\textrm{ is symmetrical about }0.

(
H
3
)

\textrm{We have }b=0.

(
H
4
)

We note that
(
H

1

α

)
is weaker than
(
H

2

α

)
. Here, the Hypothesis
(\mathbf{H_{1}^{2}})
always holds because the Lévy measure
F
integrates
x\mapsto|x|^{2}\wedge 1
. That is

|\beta|^{2}\theta(\beta)=\int_{|x|>\beta}(|\beta|^{2}\wedge 1)F(dx)\leq\int_{|x|>\beta}(|x|^{2}\wedge 1)F(dx)<+\infty.

Now, let us give an example of a process in finance which satisfies the first two hypotheses.

Example
.

We consider the
CGMY
process with Lévy density

\displaystyle F(dx)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Ce^{-Mx}/x^{1+Y}&x>0\\
Ce^{Gx}/|x|^{1+Y}&x<0\end{array}\right.

where
C,G,M>0
and
0<Y<2
. For some sequence
\beta_{n}\in(0,1]
and
\beta_{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, this process always satisfies Hypothesis
(
H

2

α

)
and therefore it satisfies Hypothesis
(
H

1

α

)
. More precisely, we have

\displaystyle\beta_{n}^{Y}\theta(\beta_{n})=\frac{2C}{Y}+C\beta_{n}^{Y}\int_{x>\beta_{n}}\frac{e^{-Mx}+e^{-Gx}-2}{x^{1+Y}}dx.

So, noticing that

\displaystyle C\beta_{n}^{Y}\int_{x>\beta_{n}}\frac{e^{-Mx}+e^{-Gx}-2}{x^{1+Y}}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{Y}(e^{-M\beta_{n}}+e^{-G\beta_{n}}-2)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\rightarrow}0.

we deduce that
\beta_{n}^{Y}\theta(\beta_{n})\rightarrow\theta
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
\theta=\frac{2C}{Y}
.

In the same spirit as in Jacod
[
9
]
, we prove sharp rate
u_{n,m}
for our multilevel error
U^{n,m}

(
1.3
)
with pointing out the technical choice for the sequence
\beta_{n}
tending to
0
that truncates the small jumps. To do so, we consider five different cases depending on some reasonably general circumstances.

(C1)

If
(
H

1

α

)
is valid for some
\alpha<1
and
d:=b-\int_{|x|\leq 1}xF(dx)\neq 0
, then we choose
u_{n,m}=\frac{nm}{m-1}
and
\beta_{n}=\frac{(\log{n})^{2}}{n}
. (See the Remark
2.3
for the finiteness of
d
)

(C2)

If
(
H

2

α

)
is valid for some
\alpha<1
and hypotheses
(
H3
)
and
(
H4
)
are also valid then we choose
u_{n,m}=\left[\frac{mn}{(m-1)\log{n}}\right]^{1/{\alpha}}
and
\beta_{n}=\left(\frac{\log{n}}{n}\right)^{1/{\alpha}}
.

(C3)

If
(
H

2

α

)
is valid for
\alpha=1
and
F
is non-symmetric then we choose
u_{n,m}=\frac{mn}{(m-1)(\log{n})^{2}}
and
\beta_{n}=\frac{\log{n}}{n}
.

(C4)

If
(
H

2

α

)
is valid for
\alpha=1
and hypothesis
(
H3
)
is also valid then we choose
u_{n,m}=\frac{mn}{(m-1)\log{n}}
and
\beta_{n}=\frac{\log{n}}{n}
.

(C5)

If
(
H

2

α

)
is valid for some
\alpha>1
then we choose
u_{n,m}=\left[\frac{mn}{(m-1)\log{n}}\right]^{1/{\alpha}}
and
\beta_{n}=\frac{\log{n}}{n^{1/(2\alpha)}}
.

2.1.
Some estimates on Lévy measure

In what follows we consider the same notations as in Jacod
[
9
]
and for
\beta>0
, we denote

\displaystyle c(\beta)=\int_{|x|\leq\beta}|x|^{2}F(dx),

\displaystyle d_{+}(\beta)=\int_{x>\beta}|x|F(dx),\hskip 28.45274ptd_{-}(\beta)=\int_{x<-\beta}|x|F(dx),

\displaystyle\rho_{+}(\beta)=\int_{x>\beta}|x|^{\alpha}F(dx),\hskip 28.45274pt\rho_{-}(\beta)=\int_{x<-\beta}|x|^{\alpha}F(dx),

(2.1)

\displaystyle\delta(\beta)=d_{+}(\beta)+d_{-}(\beta),\hskip 28.45274pt\rho(\beta)=\rho_{+}(\beta)+\rho_{-}(\beta),

\displaystyle d^{\prime}(\beta)=d_{+}(\beta)-d_{-}(\beta),\hskip 28.45274ptb^{\prime}=b+\int_{|x|>1}xF(dx),

\displaystyle d(\beta)=b^{\prime}-d^{\prime}(\beta).

Note that
d(\beta)=b-\int_{\beta<|x|\leq 1}xF(dx)
if
\beta<1
and
d(\beta)=b
if
\beta=1
.

\bullet
Without loss of generality we can reduce ourselves to study the case where we have bounded jumps and coefficient
f
with compact support. Thus, from now on we assume that

(A)

f\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
with compact support and
|\Delta Y|\leq p
for some integer
p\geq 1
, which amounts to say that
\theta(p)=0
.

Indeed, adapting the same arguments as in Proposition 2.4 in Jacod in
[
9
]
to the multilevel error setting, we can easily recover our main results namely Theorem
3.1
, Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
3.4
for non-bounded jumps and coefficient
f
without a compact support.

Remark 2.1
.

Note that under
(A)
the quantity
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{a}F(dx)
,
a\geq 2
is finite.

In this part, we recall from Jacod in
[
9
]
under assumption
(A)
some useful estimations on the above quantities introduced in
(
2.1
)
. We provide some details for the proofs of the following lemmas in appendix
B
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Since
\theta(p)=0
, under
(
H

1

α

)
, we have for any
\beta\in(0,1]

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}c(\beta)\leq C\beta^{2-\alpha},\hskip 28.45274pt\rho(\beta)\leq C\log\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right)\\
\int_{|x|>\beta}|x|^{\alpha/2}F(dx)\leq\frac{C}{\beta^{\alpha/2}}\\
\delta(\beta)+|d(\beta)|+d_{+}(\beta)+d_{-}(\beta)+|d^{\prime}(\beta)|\leq Cs(\beta)\end{array}\right.\mbox{where }s(\beta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&\alpha<1\\
\log(\frac{1}{\beta}),&\alpha=1\\
\beta^{1-\alpha},&\alpha>1\end{array}\right.

Remark 2.3
.

Note that under
(
H

1

α

)
with
\alpha<1
, we have
|\delta(\beta)|<C
for all
\beta\in(0,1]
, then by the monotone convergence theorem
\int|x|F(dx)<\infty
.

Lemma 2.4
.

If further
(
H

2

α

)
holds, then we obtain the following equivalences or convergences as
\beta
goes to
0
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}c(\beta)\sim\frac{\alpha\theta}{2-\alpha}\beta^{2-\alpha},&&\\
\frac{\rho_{+}(\beta)}{\log{1/{\beta}}}\rightarrow\alpha\theta_{+}&\frac{\rho_{-}(\beta)}{\log{1/{\beta}}}\rightarrow\alpha\theta_{-},&\\
\beta^{\alpha-1}d_{+}(\beta)\rightarrow\frac{\alpha\theta_{+}}{\alpha-1}&\beta^{\alpha-1}d_{-}(\beta)\rightarrow\frac{\alpha\theta_{-}}{\alpha-1}&\textrm{if }\alpha>1\\
\frac{d_{+}(\beta)}{\log{(1/\beta)}}\rightarrow\theta_{+}&\frac{d_{-}(\beta)}{\log{(1/\beta)}}\rightarrow\theta_{-}&\textrm{if }\alpha=1\\
d_{+}(\beta)\rightarrow d_{+}&d_{-}(\beta)\rightarrow d_{-}&\textrm{if }\alpha<1\end{array}\right.

with some positive constants
d_{+}
and
d_{-}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

When
\alpha=1
, under assumption
(
H

2

α

)
, we have for every
b>0
and as
\beta\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\log{(1/\beta)})^{2}}\int_{\beta<|x|\leq b}(x\log{|x|})F(dx)\rightarrow-\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2}.

(2.13)

Besides, for a given truncating sequence
(\beta_{n})_{n\geq 0}
that tends to zero as
n
tends to infinity, we introduce

\displaystyle\boxed{c_{n}:=c(\beta_{n}),\;\;d_{n}:=d(\beta_{n}),\;\;d^{\prime}_{n}:=d^{\prime}(\beta_{n}),\;\;\rho_{n}:=\rho(\beta_{n})\;\;\mbox{ and }\delta_{n}:=\delta(\beta_{n}),}

to make the notations less cluttered.
We deduce easily from
(
2.2
)
that under
(
H

1

α

)
, we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}c_{n}\leq C\beta_{n}^{2-\alpha},\\
{d^{\prime}}_{n}+|d_{n}|+\delta_{n}\leq Cs(\beta_{n}),\\
\rho_{n}\leq C\log\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{n}}\right).\\
\end{array}\right.

2.2.
Semimartingale decomposition

Now, we give a decomposition of the process
Y
.

\bullet
For a predictable real function
\delta
on
{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and a real measure
m
, we denote
\delta\ast m
the stochastic integral process given by
\delta\ast m_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta(s,x)m(ds,dx)
for
t\geq 0
.
Let
\mu
denotes the jump measure of our driving Lévy process
Y
and
\nu(ds,dx)=ds\otimes F(dx)
is its predictable compensator. For
\beta>0
, we can write

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=Y^{\beta}+N^{\beta},\;\mbox{ where }\,Y^{\beta}=A^{\beta}+M^{\beta}\;\mbox{ with }

(2.17)

\displaystyle A_{t}^{\beta}

\displaystyle=d(\beta)t,\;\;\;M_{t}^{\beta}=x\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\leq\beta\}}\ast(\mu-\nu)_{t}\;\;\mbox{ and }\;N_{t}^{\beta}=x\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|>\beta\}}\ast\mu_{t}.

Then
M^{\beta}
is a square-integrable martingale with predictable bracket
<M^{\beta},M^{\beta}>_{t}=c(\beta)t
. Moreover under assumption
(A)
, for
\beta\geq p
we have
N^{\beta}=0
and then
Y=A^{\beta}+M^{\beta}
with
A_{t}^{\beta}=b^{\prime}t
, whereas for
\beta=1
we have
A^{1}_{t}=bt
.

\bullet
In the context of the multilevel type error
(
1.3
)
, we consider two time discretization grids. The coarse grid with time step
1/n
and with associated times
t_{i}:=\frac{i-1}{n}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
. The finer grid with time step
1/nm
and with associated times

t_{i}^{k}:=\frac{m(i-1)+k-1}{nm},

with
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
k\in\{1,\dots,m+1\}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
.
Note that
t_{1}^{1}=t_{0}
and
t_{i}^{1}=t_{i}
corresponding to the coarser grid. We note also that the point of the coarse grid can be written either as a final point
t_{i-1}^{m+1}
or as the next point
t_{i}^{1}
on the same grid.
Now, for a given truncating sequence
(\beta_{n})_{n\geq 0}
that tends to zero as
n\to\infty
, we denote

\boxed{M^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k},t}=M_{t}^{\beta_{n}}-M_{t_{i}^{k}}^{\beta_{n}}}\;\mbox{ for }t\in I(nm,i,k):=(t_{i}^{k},t_{i}^{k+1}],

with
(i,k)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\{1,\dots,m\}
. Further, let
\Big(T^{\beta_{n}}_{p}({t_{i}^{k}})\Big)_{p\geq 1}
denotes the sequence of the successive jump times of
Y
after time
t_{i}^{k}
and of size bigger than or equal to
\beta_{n}
. Let also
K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})
denotes the random number of jumps occurring in the time interval
I(nm,i,k)
that satisfies
T^{\beta_{n}}_{\scriptsize K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})}{(t_{i}^{k})}\leq t_{i}^{k+1}<T^{\beta_{n}}_{\scriptsize K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})+1}{(t_{i}^{k})}
. Note that the random number
K(t_{i}^{k})
is well-defined as we use the cut-off of size
\beta_{n}
. Then, the following two main properties hold:

(P1)

Conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}^{k}}
, the random variables
(\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{\scriptsize p}(t_{i}^{k})})_{p\geq 1}
,
K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})
and
\{M^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k},t},t\in I(nm,i,k)\}
are independent. Each
\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{\scriptsize p}(t_{i}^{k})}
has the density
\frac{1}{\theta(\beta_{n})}1_{|x|>\beta_{n}}F(dx)
and
K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})
has Poisson law with parameter

\boxed{\lambda_{n,m}=\frac{\theta(\beta_{n})}{nm}.}

(P2)

The process
\{M^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k},t},t\in I(nm,i,k)\}
is a Lévy process, independent of
\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}^{k}}
and satisfying for
t\in I(nm,i,k)
, for
v\in\mathbb{R}

\mathds{E}(e^{\texttt{i}vM^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k},t}})=\exp\left\{\left(t-{t_{i}^{k}}\right)\int_{|y|\leq\beta_{n}}(e^{\texttt{i}vy}-1-\texttt{i}vy)F(dy)\right\}.

We also note that under Hypothesis
(
H

1

α

)
, with some choice
\beta_{n}
going to
0
, we have

\lambda_{n,m}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

3.
Main results

Our main results are to prove the convergence in law of

u_{n,m}U^{n,m}
to the limit process with the above choices of the rate
u_{n,m}
corresponding to those cases. First of all, we assume that function
f
always satisfies assumption
(
Hf
)
. The theorem below is about the tightness which can be easily deduced by Lemma
4.5
, Lemma
4.4
, Lemma
4.8
, Lemma
4.7
, Lemma
4.11
, Lemma
4.10
, Lemma
4.14
, Lemma
4.13
, Corollary
4.3
and Lemma
C.2
in appendix
C
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Assume that hypothesis
(
H

1

α

)
holds for some
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Then, with the above choice of
u_{n,m}
in the previous section, the sequence
(u_{n,m}U^{n,m})
is tight.

Let
\overline{Y}^{n}
be the discretized process associated with
Y
, that is
\overline{Y}^{n}_{t}=Y_{\eta_{n}(t)}
. We observe that the sequence
\overline{Y}^{n}
converges pointwise to the process
Y
for the Skorohod topology. The following limit theorem considering the error between two consecutive levels Euler approximations is covered by
(C1)
.

Theorem 3.2
.

For case
(C1)
, the sequence

(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}{U}^{n,m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(Y,U),\textrm{ when }n\rightarrow\infty

where
U
is the unique solution of the linear equation

U_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}f^{\prime}(X_{s-})U_{s-}dY_{s}-Z_{t},\quad t\in[0,1]

(3.1)

and when letting
n\rightarrow\infty
,

Z_{t}=d\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n:R_{n}\leq t\end{subarray}}\left(ff^{\prime}(X_{R_{n}-})\Delta Y_{R_{n}}\frac{\lfloor m\Upsilon_{n}\rfloor}{m-1}+(f(X_{R_{n}})-f(X_{R_{n}-}))(1-\frac{\lfloor m\Upsilon_{n}\rfloor}{m-1})\right)\\
+\frac{d^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}f(X_{s-})f^{\prime}(X_{s-})ds

(3.2)

where
d=b-\int_{|x|\leq 1}xF(dx)
,
(R_{n})_{n\geq 1}
denotes an enumeration of the jump times of
Y
(or of
X
) and
(\Upsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of i.i.d. variables, uniform on
[0,1]
and independent of
Y
.

Proof.

This proof uses some results in Section
4
. In particular, we begin with the decomposition of
u_{n,m}U^{n,m}
from
(
4.2
)
. From Theorem
4.6
and Lemma
4.4
, we have
(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}{Z}^{n,m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(Y,Z)
. Then the result is straight-forward according to Theorem
4.2
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

From Theorem
3.2
, if we let
m\rightarrow\infty
, we will recover the same limit as the case of Euler (Case 3a in Jacod’s paper
[
9
]
), that is

\displaystyle Z_{t}=

\displaystyle d\sum_{n:R_{n}\leq t}([f(X_{R_{n}})-f(X_{R_{n}-})]\Upsilon_{n}+f^{\prime}(X_{R_{n}-})\Delta X_{R_{n}}(1-\Upsilon_{n}))+\frac{d^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}f(X_{s-})f^{\prime}(X_{s-})ds,

where
d=b-\int_{|x|\leq 1}xF(dx)
and
(\Upsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of i.i.d. variables, uniform on
[0,1]
and independent of
Y
, and
(R_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is an enumeration of the jump times of
Y
(or of
X
).

The following limit theorem is of the rest cases and with stronger assumption
(
H

2

α

)
.

Theorem 3.4
.

We have that the sequence

(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}U^{n,m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(Y,U),\textrm{ when }n\rightarrow\infty

where
U
is the unique solution of the linear equation
(
3.1
)

and the process
Z
is described as follows:

(a)

In
(C2)
and
(C4)
, we have
(
3.5
)
, where
V
is another Lévy process, independent of
Y
and characterized by

\mathds{E}(e^{iuV_{t}})=\exp\left(\frac{t\theta^{2}\alpha}{4}\int\frac{1}{|x|^{1+\alpha}}(e^{iux}-1-iux\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\leq 1\}})dx\right).

(3.3)

Hence,
V
is a symmetric stable process with index
\alpha
.

(b)

In
(C3)
,

Z_{t}=-\frac{{\theta^{\prime}}^{2}}{4}\int_{0}^{t}f(X_{s-})f^{\prime}(X_{s-})ds,

(3.4)

and we even have that
u_{n,m}U^{n,m}
converges to
U
in probability (locally uniformly in time).

(c)

In
(C5)
,

Z_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}f(X_{s-})f^{\prime}(X_{s-})dV_{s}

(3.5)

where
V
is a Lévy process, independent of
Y
and characterized by

\mathds{E}(e^{iuV_{t}})=\exp\left(\frac{t\alpha}{2}\int\left\{\left[(\theta_{+}^{2}+\theta_{-}^{2})\mathbbm{1}_{\{x>0\}}+2\theta_{+}\theta_{-}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x<0\}}\right]\times\frac{1}{|x|^{1+\alpha}}(e^{iux}-1-iux)\right\}dx\right).

(3.6)

Hence,
V
is stable with index
\alpha
.

Proof.

This proof also uses some of the results in Section
4
. In particular, we begin with the decomposition of
u_{n,m}U^{n,m}
from
(
4.2
)
. From Theorem
4.9
, Theorem
4.12
, Theorem
4.15
, Lemma
4.7
, Lemma
4.10
and Lemma
4.13
, we have
(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}{Z}^{n,m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(Y,Z)
. Then the result is straight-forward according to Theorem
4.2
.
∎

4.
Error analysis

As mentioned in subsection
2.1
, with no loss of generality, we develop our error analysis under assumption
(A)
. For
t\in[0,1]
, we first recall that
\eta_{n}(t)=\frac{[nt]}{n}
. The error between two consecutive levels Euler approximations
U^{n,m}_{t}:=X^{n}_{\eta_{nm}(t)}-X^{nm}_{\eta_{nm}(t)}
is given by

\displaystyle U^{n,m}_{\eta_{n}(t)}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}\Big(f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{n})-f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{nm})\Big)dY_{s}-\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}\Big(f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{n})-f(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})\Big)dY_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}\Big(f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{nm}+U^{n,m}_{s-})-f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{nm})\Big)dY_{s}

\displaystyle\hskip 190.63338pt-\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}\Big(f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{n})-f(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})\Big)dY_{s}.

Let
G(x,y):=f(x+yf(x))-f(x)
, using the interpolation Euler scheme
X^{n}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}=X^{n}_{\eta_{n}(s-)}+f(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle U^{n,m}_{\eta_{n}(t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}\Big(f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{nm}+U^{n,m}_{s-})-f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{nm})\Big)dY_{s}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt-\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}G(X^{n}_{\eta_{n}(s-)},Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dY_{s}.

(4.1)

Remark 4.1
.

Under assumption
(A)
, using Taylor’s expansion we write

G(x,y)=yff^{\prime}(x)+y^{2}k(x,y),

where
k
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function that vanishes outside
K\times\mathbb{R}
for some compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}
. Also, we note that
ff^{\prime}
has compact support.

Consequently, given a deterministic rate of convergence
u_{n,m}
, we write

\displaystyle u_{n,m}U^{n,m}_{\eta_{n}(t)}=

\displaystyle u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}\Big(f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{nm}+U^{n,m}_{s-})-f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{nm})\Big)dY_{s}-u_{n,m}Z^{n,m}_{t},

(4.2)

where

Z^{n,m}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dY_{s}\\
+\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}k(X^{n}_{\eta_{n}(s-)},Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})(Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})^{2}dY_{s}.

(4.3)

Now, recalling the notation
\overline{Y}^{n}_{t}=Y_{\eta_{n}(t)}
, for
t\in[0,1]
, we follow the same arguments as in
[
7
, Theorem 9.3 page 40]
to prove the following result.

Proposition 4.2
.

For
n\to\infty
, if
(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}Z^{n,m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\rightarrow}}(Y,Z)
then
(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}U^{n,m}_{\eta_{n}(.)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\rightarrow}}(Y,U)
, where the limiting process
U
is solution to
(
3.1
)
.

Proof.

By
(
4.2
)
, we have

\hskip-11.38092ptu_{n,m}U^{n,m}_{\eta_{n}(t)}=\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}\frac{f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{nm}+U^{n,m}_{s-})-f(X_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}^{nm})}{U^{n,m}_{s-}}(u_{n,m}U^{n,m}_{s-})\mathbbm{1}_{\{U^{n,m}_{s-}\neq 0\}}dY_{s}-u_{n,m}Z^{n,m}_{t}.

Let
T^{n,a}=\inf{\{t>0:|u_{n,m}U^{n,m}_{t}|>a\}}
. Then the sequence
(u_{n,m}U_{t\wedge T^{n,a}}^{n,m})
is relatively compact, and any limit point will satisfy
(
3.1
)
on
[0,T^{a}]
, where
T^{a}=\inf{\{t>0:|U_{t}|>a\}}
. But
\lim_{a\uparrow\infty}T^{a}=\infty
a.s., so
u_{n,m}U^{n,m}_{\eta_{n}(.)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\rightarrow}}U
.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

The tightness of the sequence
(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}U^{n,m}_{\eta_{n}(.)})
is a straight-forward consequence of the tightness of the sequence
(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}Z^{n,m})
.

Thus, the aim now is to study the asymptotic behavior of the couple
(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}Z^{n,m})
. To do so, on the one hand, we set

u_{n,m}Z^{n,m}_{t}=:\mathcal{M}^{n,m}_{t}+\mathcal{R}^{n,m}_{t},

(4.4)

where
\mathcal{M}^{n,m}
stands for the main term contributing in the limit behavior and

\mathcal{R}^{n,m}_{t}
stands for the rest term that will tends to zero. In the sequel, the expression of the above decomposition has to be specified for each case
(C1)
-
(C5)
.
It is worth noticing that the second term in
(
4.3
)
will not contribute in the limit and will be considered as a part of
\mathcal{R}^{n,m}
except for
(C1)
where it will be considered as a part of
{\mathcal{M}}^{n,m}
. On the other hand, we also need to rewrite the process
\overline{Y}^{n}
in a triangular array form. To do so, recalling our notations given in subsection
2.2
, with the formula
(
2.17
)
and taking into account the number of jumps
K(t_{i}^{k})
occuring in the time interval
I(nm,i,k)
, with a truncating sequence
(\beta_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, we write

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cl}\overline{Y}^{n}:=\overline{Y}^{n}(1)+\overline{Y}^{n}(2),&\mbox{where}\\
\overline{Y}^{n}_{t}(1)=\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}y^{n,m}_{i}(1),&y^{n,m}_{i}(1):=\sum_{k=1}^{m}(M^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k},t_{i}^{k+1}}+\sum_{j\geq 2}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{\scriptsize j}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})\geq j\}}),\\
\overline{Y}^{n}_{t}(2)=\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}y^{n,m}_{i}(2),&y^{n,m}_{i}(2):=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{d_{n}}{nm}+\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{\scriptsize 1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})\geq 1\}}\right).\end{array}

In particular,
(\overline{Y}^{n}(1))_{n\geq 0}
converges uniformly in probability to zero for all cases except case
(C3)
and
(\overline{Y}^{n}(2))_{n\geq 0}
is tight for all cases.
Each subsection below is dedicated to study separately each case. Note that from now on, we denote
C
as some positive generic constant that can be changed from line to line. Moreover, by the notation
\Gamma^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathds{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
, we mean that
\sup_{s\leq t}|\Gamma_{s}^{n}|
goes to
0
in probability for all
t
as
n
tends to infinity.

4.1.
Asymptotic behavior of the couple
(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}Z^{n,m})
for case
(C1)
.

For this subsection, we first need to introduce some complementary notations. Following subsection
2.1
,
let
(T^{\beta_{n}}_{\scriptsize p}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}))_{p\geq 1}
denotes the sequence of jump times and
K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})
the random number of jumps that occur on the interval
(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}]
, where we recall that
t_{i}^{1}=\frac{i-1}{n}
and
t_{i}^{k}=\frac{m(i-1)+k-1}{nm}
.
Then, each
\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{\scriptsize p}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})}
has the density
\frac{1}{\theta(\beta_{n})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|>\beta_{n}\}}F(dx)
and
K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})
has a Poisson distribution with parameter
(k-1)\lambda_{n,m}
. By the representation formula
(
2.17
)
, we obtain a decomposition for the normalized error term
u_{n,m}Z^{n,m}_{t}
. More precisely, from
(
4.3
)
, we write

\displaystyle u_{n,m}Z^{n,m}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{5}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})d\Gamma_{s}^{n}(i)

\displaystyle+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1)+\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}k(X^{n}_{\eta_{n}(s-)},Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})d\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{s}(3)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(4)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(5),

where

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\Gamma^{n}_{t}(1)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{t}(A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dA^{\beta_{n}}_{s},\\
\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{t}(A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dN^{\beta_{n}}_{s}+u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{t}(N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dA^{\beta_{n}}_{s},\\
\Gamma^{n}_{t}(3)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{t}(M^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-M^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dY_{s},\\
\Gamma^{n}_{t}(4)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{t}(N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dN^{\beta_{n}}_{s},\\
\Gamma^{n}_{t}(5)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{t}[(A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})+(N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})]dM^{\beta_{n}}_{s},\\
\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1)=&\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}k(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})})(N^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k}}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{1}})^{2},\\
\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(2)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{t}(N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})^{2}dA^{\beta_{n}}_{s},\\
\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(3)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{t}(Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})^{2}dY_{s}-\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(2),\\
\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(4)=&\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}(k(X^{n}_{\eta_{n}(s-)},Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})-k(X^{n}_{\eta_{n}(s-)},N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)}))d\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{s}(2),\\
\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(5)=&\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}(k(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}},N^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k}}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{1}})-k(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})}))(N^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k}}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{1}})^{2}.\end{array}\right.

Now, we rewrite
\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1)=\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,1)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,2),

where

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,1)

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}k(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})})(\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})})^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})\geq 1\}},

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,2)

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}k(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})})(\sum_{h=2}^{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})}(\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{h}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})})^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h,h^{\prime}=2\\
h\neq h^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{h}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{h^{\prime}}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})}),

with the convention
\sum\limits_{i}^{j}
for
j<i
equals to zero. For the term driven by
\Gamma^{n}(2)
, we rewrite

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})d\Gamma^{n}_{s}(2)

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,1)+\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,2)+\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,3),

where

\displaystyle\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,1)

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n})\left[(k-1)\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})\geq 1\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})=0\}}+\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})\geq 1\}}\right],

\displaystyle\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,2)

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n})\Big[(k-1)\sum_{h=2}^{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{h}(t_{i}^{k})}+\sum_{h=2}^{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{h}(t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k})}\Big],

\displaystyle\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,3)

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n})(k-1)\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{k}})\geq 1\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})\geq 1\}}.

Then we rewrite
\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,1)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,1):=\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i}(1)+\tilde{z}^{n}_{i}(2))
where

\displaystyle\tilde{z}^{n}_{i}(1)=

\displaystyle\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}})(k-1)\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K({\scriptsize t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{k}})=0\}},

\displaystyle\tilde{z}^{n}_{i}(2)=

\displaystyle\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{k=2}^{m}G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}.

Now, concerning
\tilde{z}^{n}_{i}(2)
, we rewrite the sum
\sum_{k=2}^{m}G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}
as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{m}G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i})\geq 1\}}+\sum_{k=2}^{m}G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}1_{K(t^{1}_{i})=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m-1)G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i})\geq 1\}}+\sum_{k=2}^{m}G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i})=0\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{2}_{i})\geq 1\}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=2}^{m}G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i})=0\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{2}_{i})=0\}}

As
K(t^{k}_{i})=K(t^{k}_{i},t_{i}^{k+1})
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, the first term in the first for
k=2
vanishes and the 2 first terms in the second sum vanishes also. Then, we have

\sum_{k=2}^{m}G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}=(m-1)G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i})\geq 1\}}\\
+(m-2)G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{2}_{i})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t^{2}_{i})=0\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{2}_{i})\geq 1\}}+\sum_{k=4}^{m}G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t^{2}_{i})=0\}}.

Then, by induction, we deduce

\tilde{z}^{n}_{i}(2)=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}(m-k)G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{k}_{i})})\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t^{k}_{i})=0\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{k}_{i})\geq 1\}}.

Therefore, we can rewrite
\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,1)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,1)=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}{\tilde{z}}_{i,k}^{n},
where

{\tilde{z}}_{i,k}^{n}=[ff^{\prime}(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}})(k-1)\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{k}_{i})}+(m-k)G(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})})]\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})=0\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}.

(4.8)

In this case, we have
u_{n,m}Z^{n,m}_{t}=\mathcal{M}^{n,m}_{t}+\mathcal{R}^{n,m}_{t},
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{n,m}_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})d\Gamma_{s}^{n}(1)+{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(2,1)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,1)\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{n,m}_{t}

\displaystyle={\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(2,2)+{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(2,3)+\sum_{i=3}^{5}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})d\Gamma_{s}^{n}(i)

(4.9)

\displaystyle+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,2)+\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}k(X^{n}_{\eta_{n}(s-)},Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})d\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{s}(3)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(4)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(5).

The proof of the following lemma is postponed in Appendix
A
below.

Lemma 4.4
.

For case
(C1)
, we have as
n\rightarrow\infty

the sequences of processes
(\overline{Y}^{n}(1))_{n\geq 0}
and
(\mathcal{R}^{n,m})_{n\geq 0}
converge uniformly in probability to
0
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For case
(C1)
, we have the sequences
(\overline{Y}^{n}(2))_{n\geq 0}
and
({\mathcal{M}}^{n,m})_{n\geq 0}
are tight.

Proof.

First, we consider
\overline{Y}^{n}_{t}(2)
given by
(
4
)
.
By the Property
(P1)
, the relation
(
2.1
)
in particular
d_{n}=b^{\prime}-d^{\prime}_{n}
, the fact that
1-\lambda_{n,m}-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}\leq\frac{\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}(y^{n}_{i}(2)|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{d_{n}}{n}+(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})\frac{{d^{\prime}}_{n}}{n\lambda_{n,m}}\right|\leq C\frac{1+\lambda_{n,m}|{d^{\prime}}_{n}|}{n}.

(4.10)

Further, by the Jensen’s inequality,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{2}F(dx)<+\infty
(see Remark
2.1
) and the inequality
1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}\leq\lambda_{n,m}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(|y^{n}_{i}(2)|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle\leq C(\frac{d_{n}^{2}}{n^{2}}+(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})\frac{1}{n\lambda_{n,m}})\leq C\frac{1}{n}(\frac{d_{n}^{2}}{n}+1).

(4.11)

Then, in case
(C1)
, using the boundedness of
|d^{\prime}_{n}|
and
|d_{n}|
(see
(
2.2
)
),
\lambda_{n,m}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
,
y^{n}_{i}(2)
satisfies
(
C.2
)
and
(
C.9
)
ensuring the tightness from the second part of Lemma
C.2
.

Now, we consider the tightness of the sequence
(\mathcal{M}^{n,m})_{n\geq 0}
. In this case, we recall that

\mathcal{M}^{n,m}_{t}=\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})d\Gamma_{s}^{n}(1)+\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,1)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,1).

We rewrite

\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})d\Gamma_{s}^{n}(1)=\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\zeta_{i}^{n}(1),

\zeta_{i}^{n}(1)=ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{k}}^{n})\frac{m(m-1)u_{n,m}d_{n}^{2}}{2n^{2}m^{2}},

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{t}(1,1)=\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}z_{i}^{n}(1),&z_{i}^{n}(1)=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{k=2}^{m}k(X^{n}_{t^{1}_{i}},\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})(\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})})^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}},\\
\Gamma^{n}_{t}(2,1)=\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}z_{i}^{n}(2),&z_{i}^{n}(2)=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n})\left[(k-1)\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})=0\}}\right.\\
&\left.\hskip 199.16928pt+\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\right].\end{array}

By similar arguments and the fact that the functions
ff^{\prime}
and
k
are bounded, we have
\mathbb{E}(|\zeta_{i}^{n}(1)||\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})\leq C\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}^{2}}{n^{2}}
,
\mathbb{E}(|z_{i}^{n}(1)||\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})\leq C\frac{u_{n,m}|d_{n}|}{n^{2}}
and
\mathbb{E}(|z_{i}^{n}(2)||\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})\leq C\frac{u_{n,m}|d_{n}|\delta_{n}}{n^{2}}
.
Then, we are in case
(C1)
, using the boundedness
|d_{n}|
and
\delta_{n}
(see
(
2.2
)
) and
u_{n,m}=\frac{nm}{m-1}
, we obtain that
\zeta_{i}^{n}(1)
,
z_{i}^{n}(1)
and
z_{i}^{n}(2)
satisfy
(
C.2
)
ensuring the tightness from Lemma
C.2
.
∎

Theorem 4.6
.

For case
(C1)
, we have

(\overline{Y}^{n},{\mathcal{M}}^{n,m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(Y,Z),

where
Z
is the limit process given in
(
3.2
)
.

Proof.

Since
ff^{\prime}
is Lipschitz-continuous, by virtue of Lemma
C.10
, in order to prove the convergence in law of
(\overline{Y}^{n},{\mathcal{M}}^{n,m})
it suffices to prove the convergence of
(\overline{Y}^{n}_{1}(2),\Gamma^{n}_{1}(1),\Gamma^{n}_{1}(2,1)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{1}(1,1))
.

First as
u_{n,m}=\frac{nm}{m-1}
, we use
(
2.2
)
to get
d_{n}\searrow d=b-\int_{|x|<1}xF(dx)
and then we have

\displaystyle\Gamma^{n}_{1}(1)=u_{n,m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=2}^{m}(A^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k}}-A^{\beta_{n}}_{t^{1}_{i}})(A^{\beta_{n}}_{t^{k+1}_{i}}-A^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k}})=\frac{(m-1)u_{n,m}d_{n}^{2}}{2mn}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{d^{2}}{2}.

Thus, it is enough to prove the convergence in law of the pair
(\overline{Y}^{n}_{1}(2),\Gamma^{n}_{1}(2,1)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{1}(1,1))
. To do so, we recall that
\Gamma^{n}_{1}(2,1)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{1}(1,1)=\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\tilde{z}}_{i,k}^{n},
where
{\tilde{z}}_{i,k}^{n}
is given by
(
4.8
)
.
Now, following Jacod
[
9
]
, we first study the above triangular array with freezing the component
X_{t^{1}_{i}}^{n}
. Thus, we treat the triangular array with

\displaystyle{\tilde{z}}^{n}_{i,k}(z)=[ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}+(m-k)G(z,\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})})]\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})=0\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}},

i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
with an arbitrary value
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Next, based on the denotations in
(
4
)
, we introduce
\overline{Y^{\prime}}^{n}_{t}(2)=\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2)
, where
{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2)=\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}
. In this case, we observe that
\overline{Y}^{n}_{1}(2)-\overline{Y^{\prime}}^{n}_{1}(2)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{d_{n}}{nm}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}d
and
\frac{u_{n,m}d_{n}}{nm}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{d}{(m-1)}.

Then instead of working with
(\overline{Y}^{n}_{1}(2),\Gamma^{n}_{1}(2,1)+\overline{\Gamma}^{n}_{1}(1,1))
, it is enough to prove the convergence in law of
((\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\tilde{z}}_{i,k}^{n}(z)),k\in\{1,\ldots,m\})\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
to
m
independent
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-Lévy processes.
First, since this
(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
-vector is tight, it is enough to prove that every weakly convergent subsequence has the same limit. In what follows, we omit the notation for the subsequence for more readability. For the independence of the components of the limit vector, by using Ex.12.8-12.10 in
[
15
]
, we only need to prove the independence between the limit marginals of
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(2))
,
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z))
and
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{z}_{i,k}^{n}(z),\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z))
, for any
k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,m\},k\not=k^{\prime}
.

\bullet
First, by the independent structure of the subsequence marginals
\sum_{i=1}^{n}({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),{y^{\prime}}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(2))
for any
k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,m\},k\not=k^{\prime}
, it is obvious that the limit marginals are independent.

\bullet
Second, for fixed
k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,m\},k\not=k^{\prime}
, we consider the sequence
\sum_{i=1}^{n}({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z))
whose variables
({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z))
,
{1\leq i\leq n}
are i.i.d. For
k>k^{\prime}
,
{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2)
and
\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z)
are obviously independent and the independence of the limit marginals is straightforward. For
k<k^{\prime}
, we use Lemma
C.7
to identify the limit characteristics. Let us denote the law of
({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z))
by
K^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}
.
We will study the convergence of
nK^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}(h)
to
K^{k,k^{\prime}}(h)
with some function
h
to be precised later where

\displaystyle K^{k,k^{\prime}}(h):=\frac{1}{m}\int h(x,0)F(dx)+\frac{1}{m}\int h(0,ff^{\prime}(z)(k^{\prime}-1)y+(m-k^{\prime})G(z,y))F(dy).

Therefore, we have
nK^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}(h)=n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z))|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})=0,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z));K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1,K(t^{1}_{i},t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})=0,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1,K(t^{1}_{i},t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k^{\prime}}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ne^{-2\lambda_{n,m}}h(0,0)+e^{-\lambda_{n,m}(k^{\prime}-1)}\frac{1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}h(0,ff^{\prime}(z)(k^{\prime}-1)y+(m-k^{\prime})G(z,y))F(dy)

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{P}(K(t_{i}^{k})=0,K(t^{1}_{i},t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})h(0,0)

\displaystyle+e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}h(x,0)F(dx)+\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}h(x,0)F(dx).

Three following headings demonstrate three elements in Lemma
C.7
, each corresponding to some specific choices of function
h
.

Concerning assertion (i)
:

Since we work with bounded jumps, we observe that
|ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x)|\leq C|x|
and
|ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x|\leq C|x|
on
x\in[-p,p]
. We shall choose
h=h_{u,v}
where
h_{u,v}(x,y)=\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\geq u,|y|\geq v\}}
bounded and vanishing on a neighborhood of
0
, with
(u,v)\not=(0,0)
. For any function
P
satisfying
|P(y)|\leq C|y|
, if we have
C|y|\leq v
then
|P(y)|\leq v
, which yields
h_{u,v}(x,P(y))=h_{u,v}(x,P(y)){\mathbbm{1}}_{\{C|y|>v\}}
for any
x,y\in[-p,p]
.
Then, we have
nK^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}(h_{u,v})\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}K^{k,k^{\prime}}({h_{u,v}})
.

Concerning assertion (ii)
:

For this case, we choose
h={h^{\prime}},{h^{\prime\prime}}
where
{h^{\prime}}(x,y)=x\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
and
{h^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)=y\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
. As
|ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x)|\leq C|x|
and
|ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x|\leq C|x|
on
x\in[-p,p]
, using the dominated convergence theorem,
\int|x|F(dx)<C
and
\frac{\theta(\beta_{n})}{n}=m\lambda_{n,m}
converges to
0
as
n
tends to infinity, we obtain that

nK^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h^{\prime}})
and
nK^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h^{\prime\prime}})
converge respectively to
K^{k,k^{\prime}}({h^{\prime}})
and
K^{k,k^{\prime}}({h^{\prime\prime}})
when
n
goes to infinity.

Concerning assertion (iii)
:

Here we take
h=h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
where
{h}_{1}(x,y)=x^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
,
{h}_{2}(x,y)=xy\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
and
{h}_{3}(x,y)=y^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
and apply similar arguments as in (ii), we get
nK^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h_{1}})
,
nK^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h_{2}})
and
nK^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h_{3}})
converge respectively to
K^{k,k^{\prime}}({h_{1}})
,
K^{k,k^{\prime}}({h_{2}})
and
K^{k,k^{\prime}}({h_{3}})
when
n
goes to infinity.
In conclusion, for any fixed
k<k^{\prime}
the obtained limit pair has independent marginals, since it has no Gaussian part and its Lévy measure
K^{k,k^{\prime}}
is supported on the union of the coordinate axes (see e.g. Ex.12.8 in
[
15
]
).

\bullet

Third, we consider the pair
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z),\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z))
for fixed
k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
k\not=k^{\prime}
and by symmetry it is enough to study only the case
k<k^{\prime}
. Let us denote the law of
(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z),\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z))
by
L^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}
. We will prove that
nL^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}(h)
converges to
L^{k,k^{\prime}}(h)
with some function
h
to be precised later and where

L^{k,k^{\prime}}(h):=\frac{1}{m}\int h(ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x),0)F(dx)\\
+\frac{1}{m}\int h(0,ff^{\prime}(z)(k^{\prime}-1)y+(m-k^{\prime})G(z,y))F(dy).

Therefore, we have
nL^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}(h)=n\mathbb{E}(h({\zeta}_{i,k}^{n}(z),{\zeta}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z))|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\mathbb{E}(h(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z),\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})=0,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z),\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z));K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1,K(t^{1}_{i},t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z),\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})=0,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1,K(t^{1}_{i},t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z),\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})=0,K(t^{1}_{i},t^{k}_{i})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z),\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})=0,K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z),\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1,K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h(\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z),\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1,K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ne^{-2\lambda_{n,m}}h(0,0)

\displaystyle+e^{-\lambda_{n,m}(k^{\prime}-1)}\frac{1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}h(0,ff^{\prime}(z)(k^{\prime}-1)y+(m-k^{\prime})G(z,y))F(dy)

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{P}(K(t_{i}^{k})=0,K(t^{1}_{i},t^{k^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})h(0,0)

\displaystyle+e^{-\lambda_{n,m}-\lambda_{n,m}(k-1)}\frac{1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}h(ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x),0)F(dx)

\displaystyle+ne^{-\lambda_{n,m}}(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}(k-1)})h(0,0)

\displaystyle+\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}e^{-\lambda_{n,m}(k-1)}}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}h(ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x),0)F(dx)

\displaystyle+n(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}(k-1)})h(0,0).

Now, to check the three conditions of Lemma
C.7
, we use similar arguments as in the second point above .

Concerning assertion (i)
:

Since
|ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x)|\leq C|x|
and
|ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x|\leq C|x|
on
x\in[-p,p]
, we choose
h=h_{u,v}
where
h_{u,v}(x,y)=\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\geq u,|y|\geq v\}}
bounded and vanishing on a neighborhood of
0
, with
(u,v)\not=(0,0)
. For any functions
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
satisfying
|P_{1}(x)|\leq C|x|
and
|P_{2}(y)|\leq C|y|
, we have
h_{u,v}(P_{1}(x),P_{2}(y))=h_{u,v}(P_{1}(x),P_{2}(y)){\mathbbm{1}}_{\{C|x|\geq u,C|y|\geq v\}}
for any
x,y\in[-p,p]
.
By similar arguments, we have
nL^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}(h_{u,v})\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}L^{k,k^{\prime}}({h_{u,v}})
.

Concerning assertion (ii)
:

For this case, we choose
h={h^{\prime}},{h^{\prime\prime}}
where
{h^{\prime}}(x,y)=x\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
and
{h^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)=y\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
. As
|ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x)|\leq C|x|
and
|ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x|\leq C|x|
on
x\in[-p,p]
, and applying similar arguments used in (ii) of the second point, we obtain that

nL^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h^{\prime}})
and
nL^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h^{\prime\prime}})
converge respectively to
L^{k,k^{\prime}}({h^{\prime}})
and
L^{k,k^{\prime}}({h^{\prime\prime}})
when
n
goes to infinity.

Concerning assertion (iii)
:

Here we take
{h}_{1}(x,y)=x^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
,
{h}_{2}(x,y)=xy\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
and
{h}_{3}(x,y)=y^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
and apply similar arguments as in (iii) of the second point, we get
nL^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h_{1}})
,
nL^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h_{2}})
and
nL^{k,k^{\prime}}_{n,m}({h_{3}})
converge respectively to
L^{k,k^{\prime}}({h_{1}})
,
L^{k,k^{\prime}}({h_{2}})
and
L^{k,k^{\prime}}({h_{3}})
when
n
goes to infinity. In conclusion, for any fixed
k<k^{\prime}
, the obtained limit pair has independent marginals, no Gaussian part and its Lévy measure
L^{k,k^{\prime}}
is supported on the union of the coordinate axes (see e.g. Ex.12.8 in
[
15
]
).

Hence, combining the three above points, the independence of the
m

\mathbb{R}^{2}
-Lévy limits of
\{(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\tilde{z}}_{i,k}^{n}(z)),k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}\}\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
is shown (see e.g. Ex.12.9-Ex.12.10 in
[
15
]
).
Now, we turn to identify the limit marginals of our vector.
Let us denote the law of
({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z))
by
K^{k}_{n,m}
. We will prove that
nK^{k}_{n,m}(h)
converges to
K^{k}(h)
where

\displaystyle K^{k}(h)=\frac{1}{m}\int h(x,ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x))F(dx).

Therefore, we have
nK^{k}_{n,m}(h)=n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z))|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})+n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})\geq 1|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\tilde{z}^{n}_{i,k}(z));K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{1}_{i},t_{i}^{k})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ne^{-\lambda_{n,m}}h(0,0)+\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}(k-1)})}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}h(x,0)F(dx)+

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda_{n,m}(k-1)}\frac{1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}h(x,ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x))F(dx).

By the same arguments and specific choices of function
h
as above, we easily verify the three elements in Lemma
C.7
. In this case, for any fixed
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, the obtained limit pair does not have independent marginals since its Lévy measure
K^{k}
is not supported on the union of the coordinate axes. Finally, the vector
((\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{z}_{i,k}^{n}(z)),k\in\{1,\dots,m\})
is convergent in law to
(({Y^{\prime}}_{1}^{k},V_{1}^{k}(z)),k\in\{1,\dots,m\})
and the sequence
(\overline{Y^{\prime}}^{n}_{1}(2),\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\tilde{z}_{i,k}^{n}(z))
weakly converges to
(Y_{1}-d,V_{1}(z))
where
d=b-\int_{|x|\leq 1}xF(dx)
and by independence
V_{1}(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}V^{k}_{1}(z)
with Lévy measure
K(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{1}{m}\int h(ff^{\prime}(z)(k-1)x+(m-k)G(z,x))F(dx)
, the drift part equal to
K(x\mathbbm{1}_{|x|\leq 1})
and no Gaussian part (see (ii) and (iii) right above). Since its Lévy measure can also be rewritten as
K(h)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{1}h(ff^{\prime}(z)\lfloor mu\rfloor x+(m-1-\lfloor mu\rfloor)G(z,x))F(dx)du
,
similarly to Jacod
[
9
, (5.23)]
, a possible representation of the limit process is given by

V_{1}(z)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n:R_{n}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left(ff^{\prime}(z)\lfloor m\Upsilon_{n}\rfloor\Delta Y_{R_{n}}+(m-1-\lfloor m\Upsilon_{n}\rfloor)(f(z+\Delta Y_{R_{n}}f(z))-f(z))\right)

where
(R_{n})_{n\geq 1}
denotes an enumeration of the jump times of
Y
(or of
X
) and
(\Upsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of i.i.d. variables, uniform on
[0,1]
and independent of
Y
. It is worth to note that this sum is of finite variation.
Now, as said at the beginning, we go back to consider the convergence related to our original term
\tilde{z}_{i,k}^{n}
defined in
(
4.8
)
where
z=X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n}
is no longer fixed. As
z\mapsto\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(z)
is continous, by following step by step the proof’s arguments of
[
9
, Theorem 1.2(d)]
, we obtain
(\overline{Y^{\prime}}^{n}(2),\sum_{i=1}^{[n.]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\tilde{z}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n})
converges in law to
(Y_{1}-d,V_{1})
where

V_{1}=\hskip-8.5359pt\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n:R_{n}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.69046pt[ff^{\prime}(X_{R_{n}-})\lfloor m\Upsilon_{n}\rfloor\Delta Y_{R_{n}}+(m-1-\lfloor m\Upsilon_{n}\rfloor)(f(X_{R_{n}-}+\Delta Y_{R_{n}}f(X_{R_{n}-}))-f(X_{R_{n}-}))].

This completes the proof.
∎

4.2.
Asymptotic behavior of the couple
(\overline{Y}^{n},u_{n,m}Z^{n,m})
for case
(C2)
and
(C4)
.

For the first component
\overline{Y}^{n}
, we use the same decomposition given by the relation
(
4
)
. For the second one, we consider the formula of
u_{n,m}Z^{n,m}
given in
(
4.3
)
. In these cases, the second term from this formula does not contribute to the limit. Therefore, we need only to give the analysis for its first term and by using the classical decomposition
(
2.17
)
of
Y
, we have

\displaystyle u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dY_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma^{n}_{t}(i),

(4.12)

where

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\Gamma_{t}^{n}(1)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dN^{\beta_{n}}_{s},\\
\Gamma_{t}^{n}(2)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dA^{\beta_{n}}_{s}\\
&+u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dA^{\beta_{n}}_{s}\\
&+u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dN^{\beta_{n}}_{s},\\
\Gamma_{t}^{n}(3)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(M^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-M^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dY_{s},\\
\Gamma_{t}^{n}(4)=&u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-A^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dM^{\beta_{n}}_{s}\\
&+u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}ff^{\prime}(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n})(N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{\eta_{n}(s-)})dM^{\beta_{n}}_{s}.\end{array}\right.

The three last terms in the above decomposition do not contribute on the limit. Then we only have to study
\Gamma_{t}^{n}(1)
and we seperate it into 2 terms: the first term that will be the essential term of the limit contains only the first jumps and the drifts and the second term that will be sort in the rest terms contains all the other jumps. More precisely, we rewrite

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t}^{n}(1)

\displaystyle=u_{n,m}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}\int_{I(nm,i,k)}ff^{\prime}(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}})(N^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k}}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{t^{1}_{i}})dN^{\beta_{n}}_{s}

\displaystyle=u_{n,m}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}})\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(N^{\beta_{n}}_{t^{j+1}_{i}}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{t^{j}_{i}})(N^{\beta_{n}}_{t^{k+1}_{i}}-N^{\beta_{n}}_{t_{i}^{k}})={\Gamma}_{t}^{n}(1,1)+{\Gamma}_{t}^{n}(1,2),

where

\displaystyle{\Gamma}_{t}^{n}(1,1)=

\displaystyle u_{n,m}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}})\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{j}_{i})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{j}_{i})\geq 1\}},

\displaystyle{\Gamma}_{t}^{n}(1,2)=

\displaystyle u_{n,m}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}})\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sum_{h=2}^{K(t^{j}_{i})}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{h}(t^{j}_{i})}

(4.13)

\displaystyle+u_{n,m}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}})\sum_{h=2}^{K(t_{i}^{k})}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{h}(t_{i}^{k})}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{j}_{i})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{j}_{i})\geq 1\}}

\displaystyle+u_{n,m}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=2}^{m}ff^{\prime}(X^{n}_{t_{i}^{1}})\sum_{h=2}^{K(t_{i}^{k})}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{h}(t_{i}^{k})}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sum_{h=2}^{K(t^{j}_{i})}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{h}(t^{j}_{i})}.

In this case,
u_{n,m}Z^{n,m}_{t}=\mathcal{M}^{n,m}_{t}+\mathcal{R}^{n,m}_{t},
with

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}^{n,m}_{t}={\Gamma}_{t}^{n}(1,1)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{R}^{n,m}_{t}=

\displaystyle{\Gamma}_{t}^{n}(1,2)+\sum_{i=2}^{5}\Gamma^{n}_{t}(i),

(4.14)

where
\Gamma^{n}_{t}(5)=u_{n,m}\int_{0}^{\eta_{n}(t)}k(X_{\eta_{n}(s-)}^{n},Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})(Y_{\eta_{nm}(s-)}-Y_{\eta_{n}(s-)})^{2}dY_{s}
.
The proof of the following lemma is postponed to Appendix
A
.

Lemma 4.7
.

For the cases
(C2)
and
(C4)
, we have as
n\rightarrow\infty
the sequences
(\overline{Y}^{n}(1))_{n\geq 0}
and
(\mathcal{R}^{n,m})_{n\geq 0}
converge uniformly in probability to
0
.

Lemma 4.8
.

For the cases
(C2)
and
(C4)
, the sequences
(\overline{Y}^{n}(2))_{n\geq 0}
and
({\mathcal{M}}^{n,m})_{n\geq 0}
are tight.

Proof.

First, we consider
\overline{Y}^{n}_{t}(2)
given by
(
4
)
. In these cases
(C2)
and
(C4)
, thanks to assumption
(
H3
)
, we have
d^{\prime}_{n}=0
and
|d_{n}|\leq C
. Then from
(
4.10
)
and
(
4.11
)
,
y^{n}_{i}(2)
satisfies
(
C.2
)
ensuring the tightness of
(\overline{Y}^{n}(2))_{n\geq 0}
from the second part of Lemma
C.2
.
Now, we rewrite that
{\Gamma}_{t}^{n}(1,1)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\zeta_{i,k,j}^{n}
with

\zeta_{i,k,j}^{n}=u_{n,m}ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n})\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{j}_{i})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{j}_{i})\geq 1\}}.

For
k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
fixed, by using
(
H3
)
, Property
(P1)
,
ff^{\prime}
is bounded, the inequality
1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}\leq\lambda_{n,m}
,
c(\beta)=\int_{|x|\leq\beta}x^{2}F(dx)
where
c(\beta)\leq C\beta^{2-\alpha}
(see
(
2.2
)
) and
\theta(\beta)\leq C\beta^{-\alpha}
(see
(
H

1

α

)
), we get

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbb{E}(\zeta_{i,k,j}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{|\zeta_{i,k,j}^{n}|\leq 1}|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})=ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n})u_{n,m}\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{n^{2}m^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\int_{\frac{1}{|ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n})|u_{n,m}|x|}\geq|y|>\beta_{n}}xyF(dx)F(dy)=0,\\
\mathbb{E}(|\zeta_{i,k,j}^{n}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{|\zeta_{i,k,j}^{n}|\leq 1}|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})\leq Cu_{n,m}^{2}\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{n^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}x^{2}c(\frac{1}{|ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n})|u_{n,m}|x|})F(dx)\leq C\frac{u_{n,m}^{\alpha}\rho_{n}}{n^{2}},\\
\mathbb{P}(|\zeta_{i,k,j}^{n}|>y|\mathcal{F}_{t^{1}_{i}})\leq C\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{n^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\theta(\frac{y}{|ff^{\prime}(X_{t_{i}^{1}}^{n})|u_{n,m}|x|})F(dx)\leq C\frac{u_{n,m}^{\alpha}\rho_{n}}{n^{2}y^{\alpha}},\quad\forall y>1\end{array}\right..

Then, we conclude the tightness of
({\mathcal{M}}^{n,m})_{n\geq 0}
by
\rho_{n}\leq C\log{1/\beta_{n}}
(see
(
2.1
)
), the choice
u_{n,m}=\left[\frac{nm}{(m-1)\log{n}}\right]^{1/\alpha}
with
\alpha\leq 1
, criteria
(
C.2
)
and Lemma
C.2
.
∎

Theorem 4.9
.

For cases
(C2)
and
(C4)
, we have

(\overline{Y}^{n},{\mathcal{M}}^{n,m})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(Y,Z),

where
Z
is the limit process given in
(
3.3
)
.

Proof.

Let us first introduce
{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{t}^{n}(1,1)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\zeta_{i,j,k}^{n}(1)
, where for any
1\leq k\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq k-1
,

\zeta_{i,j,k}^{n}(1)=u_{n,m}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{j}_{i})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{j}_{i})\geq 1\}}.

Since
ff^{\prime}
is Lipschitz-continuous, by virtue of Lemma
C.10
, in order to prove the convergence in law of
(\overline{Y}^{n},{\mathcal{M}}^{n,m})
it suffices to prove that
(\overline{Y}^{n}_{1}(2),{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1}^{n}(1,1))
converges in law to
(Y_{1},V_{1})
where
V
is a Lévy process independent of
Y
and characterized by
(
3.3
)
. Now, let us denote

\overline{Y^{\prime}}^{n}_{t}(2)=\sum_{i=1}^{[nt]}\sum_{k=1}^{m}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\quad\textrm{where}\quad{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2)=\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}.

From the hypothesis
(
H3
)
,
d_{n}=b
in this case, then
\overline{Y}^{n}_{1}(2)-\overline{Y^{\prime}}^{n}_{1}(2)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{d_{n}}{nm}=b
which allows us to prove instead the couple
(\overline{Y^{\prime}}^{n}_{1}(2),{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1}^{n}(1,1))
converges to the limit process
(Y_{1}-b,V_{1})
with no drift and no continuous martingale part. To do so, we choose the strategy of proving the convergence of the
\mathbb{R}^{\frac{m(m-1)}{2}+m}
-vector

\left(\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2)\right)_{k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}},\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i,j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{n}(1)\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}\\
j^{\prime}<k^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\right)

to the limit vector whose components are pairwise independent Lévy processes.
First, since this
\mathbb{R}^{\frac{m(m-1)}{2}+m}
-vector is tight thanks to Lemma
4.8
, it is enough to prove that every weakly convergent subsequence has the same limit. In what follows, we omit the notation for the subsequence for more readability. For the independence of the components of the limit vector, by using Ex.12.8-12.10 in
[
15
]
, we only need to prove the independence between the limit marginals of the pairs
((\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(2));{k\neq k^{\prime}})
,
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i,j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{n}(1))
and
((\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i,j,k}^{n}(1),\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i,j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{n}(1));(k,j)\neq(k^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
, for any
k,k^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, and
j<k
,
j^{\prime}<k^{\prime}
, then we obtain the Fourrier transform of the limit vector.

\bullet
First, by the independent structure of the subsequence marginals
\sum_{i=1}^{n}({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),{y^{\prime}}_{i,k^{\prime}}^{n}(2))
for any
k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,m\},k\not=k^{\prime}
, it is obvious that the limit marginals are i.i.d.

\bullet
Second, for fixed
k,k^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq k^{\prime}-1
we consider the convergence of the triangular array
\sum_{i=1}^{n}({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\zeta_{i,j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{n}(1))
whose generic terms
(({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\zeta_{i,j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{n}(1)))_{1\leq i\leq n}
are i.i.d. When
k,k^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}
are different, the independence between the marginals is obvious. By symmetry of the roles played by
j^{\prime}
and
k^{\prime}
in
\zeta_{i,j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{n}(1)
, it is sufficient to consider the case when
k=k^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}\neq k^{\prime}
. Note that the law of
({y^{\prime}}_{i,k}^{n}(2),\zeta_{i,j^{\prime},k}^{n}(1))
does not depend on parameters
k
and
j^{\prime}
, then we denote it by
K^{1}_{n,m}
. We will prove that
nK^{1}_{n,m}(h)
converges to
K^{1}(h)
as
n
tends to infinity for some suitable function
h
where

K^{1}(dx,dy)=\frac{1}{m}\delta_{0}(dy)F(dx)+\frac{\theta^{2}\alpha}{2m(m-1)}\delta_{0}(dx)\frac{1}{|y|^{1+\alpha}}dy

and
\delta_{0}
is Dirac measure sitting at point
0
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle nK^{1}_{n,m}(h)

\displaystyle=n\mathbb{E}(h(\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}},u_{n,m}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1\}}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{j^{\prime}}_{i})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K(t^{j^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1\}})|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}^{1}})

\displaystyle=n\mathbb{E}(h(0,0);K(t^{k}_{i})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}^{1}})+n\mathbb{E}(h(\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})},0);K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{j^{\prime}}_{i})=0|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}^{1}})

\displaystyle+n\mathbb{E}(h(\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})},u_{n,m}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t_{i}^{k})}\Delta Y_{T^{\beta_{n}}_{1}(t^{j^{\prime}}_{i})});K(t_{i}^{k})\geq 1,K(t^{j^{\prime}}_{i})\geq 1|\mathcal{F}_{t_{i}^{1}})

\displaystyle=ne^{-\lambda_{n,m}}h(0,0)+\frac{e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\hskip-17.07182pth(x,0)F(dx)

\displaystyle+\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{n(m\lambda_{n,m})^{2}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\hskip-17.07182ptF(dx)\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}\hskip-17.07182pth(x,u_{n,m}xy)F(dy).

Three following headings demonstrate three elements in Lemma
C.7
, each corresponding to some specific choices of function
h
.

Concerning assertion (i)
:

We choose
h=h_{u,v}
where
h_{u,v}(x,y)=\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\geq u,|y|\geq v\}}
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
(u,v)\neq(0,0)
.

a)

For
u>0
and
v=0
,

\displaystyle nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{u,0})=\frac{e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\hskip-14.22636pt\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\geq u\}}F(dx)+\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\hskip-14.22636pt\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\geq u\}}F(dx).

As soon as
u>\beta_{n}
, we have
\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\geq u\}}F(dx)=\theta(u-),

where
\theta(u-)
denotes the left limit at point
u
of the decreasing and right-continuous function
\theta(.)
.
Then we get
nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{u,0})\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}K^{1}(h_{u,0})=\frac{\theta(u-)}{m}
.

b)

For
u=0
and
v>0
, we have

\displaystyle nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{0,v})=\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{nm^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|u_{n,m}xy|\geq v\}}F(dy)F(dx).

Now, we denote the constant
v_{m}=v(\frac{m-1}{m})^{1/\alpha}
, using
u_{n,m}=\left[\frac{mn}{(m-1)\log{n}}\right]^{1/{\alpha}}
,
\beta_{n}=\left(\frac{\log{n}}{n}\right)^{1/{\alpha}}
and
u_{n,m}\beta_{n}=(\frac{m-1}{m})^{1/\alpha}
, then as soon as
\beta_{n}<v_{m}
, we have

\frac{1}{nm^{2}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|u_{n,m}xy|\geq v\}}F(dx)F(dy)\\
=\frac{1}{nm^{2}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\theta(\beta_{n}\vee\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx)=\frac{1}{nm^{2}}(\theta(\beta_{n})\theta(v_{m}-)+\int_{\beta_{n}<|x|\leq v_{m}}\theta(\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx)).

By
(
H

2

α

)
, the first term is equivalent to
\frac{\theta}{nm^{2}\beta_{n}^{\alpha}}\theta(v_{m}-)
which converges to
0
.
Considering the second term, let us denote
y_{n}=\frac{1}{nm^{2}}\int_{\beta_{n}<|x|\leq v_{m}}\theta(\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx)
. Let
\varepsilon>0
, by
(
H

2

α

)
, there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in(0,v_{m})
such that for
\beta\in(0,\varepsilon^{\prime})
we have
|\frac{\beta^{\alpha}\theta(\beta)}{\theta}-1|\leq\varepsilon
. Then, we denote
y_{n}=y_{n}^{1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}+y_{n}^{2,\varepsilon^{\prime}},
where
y_{n}^{1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{nm^{2}}\int_{\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}<|x|\leq v_{m}}\theta(\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx)
and
y_{n}^{2,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{nm^{2}}\int_{\beta_{n}<|x|\leq\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}\theta(\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx)
. On the one hand, by the fact that
\theta(.)
is decreasing and
(
H

1

α

)
, we have

y_{n}^{2,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{nm^{2}}\int_{\beta_{n}<|x|\leq\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}\theta(\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx)\leq\frac{\theta(\beta_{n})\theta(\varepsilon^{\prime})}{nm^{2}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

On the other hand, if we denote
{y^{\prime}}_{n}^{1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\frac{\theta}{nm^{2}v_{m}^{\alpha}\beta_{n}^{\alpha}}\int_{\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}<|x|\leq v_{m}}|x|^{\alpha}F(dx)
, thanks to
\rho_{n}\sim\alpha\theta\log{1/\beta_{n}}
(see
(
2.4
)
) we have
{y^{\prime}}_{n}^{1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\frac{\theta}{nm^{2}v_{m}^{\alpha}\beta_{n}^{\alpha}}(\rho(\frac{v_{m}\beta_{n}}{\varepsilon^{\prime}})-\rho(v_{m}-))\sim\frac{\alpha\theta^{2}}{nm^{2}v_{m}^{\alpha}\beta_{n}^{\alpha}}\log{1/\beta_{n}}
.
From
(
H

2

α

)
, we have
{y^{\prime}}_{n}^{1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(1-\varepsilon)\leq{y}_{n}^{1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\leq{y^{\prime}}_{n}^{1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(1+\varepsilon)
and since
\varepsilon
is arbitrarily small,
{y}_{n}^{1,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\sim\frac{\alpha\theta^{2}}{nm^{2}v_{m}^{\alpha}\beta_{n}^{\alpha}}\log{1/\beta_{n}}
. Then we get
nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{0,v})\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}K^{1}(h_{0,v})=\frac{\theta^{2}}{m(m-1)v^{\alpha}}
. In what follows, we will reused the obtained result

\displaystyle\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{nm^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{|x|>\beta_{n}}\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|u_{n,m}xy|\geq v\}}F(dy)F(dx)\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{\theta^{2}}{m(m-1)v^{\alpha}}.

(4.15)

c)

For
u>0
and
v>0
, as soon as
u>\beta_{n}
, by the inequality
1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}\leq\lambda_{n,m}
, we have

nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{u,v})=\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{nm^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{|x|\geq u}\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|u_{n,m}xy|\geq v\}}F(dy)F(dx)\leq\frac{\theta(u-)\theta(\beta_{n})}{nm^{2}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Then we get
nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{u,v})\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}K^{1}(h_{u,v})=0
.

Concerning assertion (ii)
:

We choose
h={h^{\prime}},{h^{\prime\prime}}
where
{h^{\prime}}(x,y)=x\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
and
{h^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)=y\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
.
Since
(
H3
)
holds, the laws
K_{n,m}^{1}
and
K^{1}
are invariant under the map
(x,y)\mapsto(-x,y)
and
(x,y)\mapsto(x,-y)
. Then in this case, we get immediately
nK_{n,m}^{1}(h)=K^{1}(h)=0.

Concerning assertion (iii)
:

Here we take
h={h}_{1},{h}_{2},{h}_{3}
where
{h}_{1}(x,y)=x^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
,
{h}_{2}(x,y)=xy\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
and
{h}_{3}(x,y)=y^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}}
. First of all, as above in (ii), by hypothesis
(
H3
)
, we have
nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{2})=K^{1}(h_{2})=0
. Now, we consider

nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{1})=\frac{e^{-\lambda_{n,m}}(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})}{m\lambda_{n,m}}\int_{\beta_{n}<|x|\leq 1}x^{2}F(dx)\\
+\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{nm^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{1\geq|x|>\beta_{n}}x^{2}F(dx)\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|y|\leq\frac{\sqrt{x^{-2}-1}}{u_{n,m}}\}}F(dy)

and

\displaystyle nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{3})=\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{nm^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{1\geq|x|>\beta_{n}}\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}u_{n,m}^{2}x^{2}y^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|y|\leq\frac{\sqrt{x^{-2}-1}}{u_{n,m}}\}}F(dx)F(dy).

Concerning the term
nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{1})
, it is clear that the first term converges to
\frac{1}{m}\int_{|x|\leq 1}x^{2}F(dx)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
and
as
\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}F(dx)<\infty
, its second term is bounded by
\frac{C\theta(\beta_{n})}{n}
which goes to
0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, we get
nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{1})\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}nK^{1}(h_{1})
. Concerning the term
nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{3})
, let
a_{m}=(\frac{m-1}{m})^{1/\alpha}
and
{a^{\prime}}_{m}=\frac{a_{m}}{\sqrt{a^{2}_{m}+1}}
, we have for
n
large enough
\beta_{n}\leq{a^{\prime}}_{m}
and

\displaystyle nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{nm^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{1\geq|x|>{a^{\prime}}_{m}}\int_{|y|>\beta_{n}}u_{n,m}^{2}x^{2}y^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|y|\leq\frac{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}a_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|}\}}F(dx)F(dy)

\displaystyle+\frac{(1-e^{-\lambda_{n,m}})^{2}}{nm^{2}\lambda_{n,m}^{2}}\int_{{a^{\prime}}_{m}\geq|x|>\beta_{n}}u_{n,m}^{2}x^{2}(c(\frac{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}a_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})-c_{n})F(dx).

Using
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1-e^{\lambda_{n,m}}}{\lambda_{n,m}}=1
and
c_{n}\leq C\beta_{n}^{2-\alpha}
, it is easy to check that the first term in the right-hand side is bounded by
C\frac{u_{n,m}^{2}c_{n}}{n}
which converges to
0
and the term
\frac{u_{n,m}^{2}}{nm^{2}}\int_{{a^{\prime}}_{m}\geq|x|>\beta_{n}}x^{2}c_{n}F(dx)
converges also to
0
. Hence, we have
nK_{n,m}^{1}(h_{3})\sim\frac{u_{n,m}^{2}}{nm^{2}}\int_{{a^{\prime}}_{m}\geq|x|>\beta_{n}}x^{2}c(\frac{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}a_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx).

Now, using
c(\beta)\sim\frac{\alpha\theta}{2-\alpha}\beta^{2-\alpha}
for
\beta\rightarrow 0
, then for
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in(0,1)
such that for
\beta\in(0,{a}_{m}\varepsilon^{\prime})
we have
|\frac{(2-\alpha)\beta^{\alpha-2}c(\beta)}{\alpha\theta}-1|\leq\varepsilon
and for
n
large enough such that
\beta_{n}<{a^{\prime}}_{m}\varepsilon^{\prime}
,
\frac{u_{n,m}^{2}}{nm^{2}}\int_{{a^{\prime}}_{m}\geq|x|>\beta_{n}}x^{2}c(\frac{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}a_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx)=x_{n}+y_{n}
where

\displaystyle x_{n}=

\displaystyle\frac{u_{n,m}^{2}}{nm^{2}}\int_{\beta_{n}<|x|\leq\beta_{n}/\varepsilon^{\prime}}x^{2}c(\frac{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}a_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx)

\displaystyle y_{n}=

\displaystyle\frac{u_{n,m}^{2}}{nm^{2}}\int_{{a^{\prime}}_{m}\geq|x|>\beta_{n}/\varepsilon^{\prime}}x^{2}c(\frac{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}a_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})F(dx).

On the one hand, for
x_{n}
, as
c(.)
is increasing, we use that
c(\frac{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}a_{m}\beta_{n}}{|x|})
is bounded to deduce an upper bound equal to
\frac{Cu_{n,m}^{2}c(\beta_{n}/\varepsilon^{\prime})}{n}
which converges to
0
. On the other hand, for
y_{n}
, if we denote
{y^{\prime}}_{n}=\frac{u_{n,m}^{2}a_{m}^{2-\alpha}\beta_{n}^{2-\alpha}}{nm^{2}}\int_{{a^{\prime}}_{m}\geq|x|>\beta_{n}/\varepsilon^{\prime}}|x|^{\alpha}(1-x^{2})^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}F(dx)
, we have
(1-\varepsilon){y^{\prime}}_{n}\leq y_{n}\leq(1+\varepsilon){y^{\prime}}_{n}
which gives
{y}_{n}\sim{y^{\prime}}_{n}
since
\varepsilon
is arbitrarily small. In what follows, we rewrite
{y^{\prime}}_{n}={y^{\prime}}_{n}^{1}+{y^{\prime}}_{n}^{2}
where

\displaystyle{y^{\prime}}_{n}^{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\theta u_{n,m}^{2}a_{m}^{2-\alpha}\beta_{n}^{2-\alpha}}{(2-\alpha)nm^{2}}\int_{{a^{\prime}}_{m}\geq|x|>\beta_{n}/\varepsilon^{\prime}}|x|^{\alpha}F(dx),

\displaystyle{y^{\prime}}_{n}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\theta u_{n,m}^{2}a_{m}^{2-\alpha}\beta_{n}^{2-\alpha}}{(2-\alpha)nm^{2}}\int_{{a^{\prime}}_{m}\geq|x|>\beta_{n}/\varepsilon^{\prime}}|x|^{\alpha}[(1-x^{2})^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}-1]F(dx).

Then by
\rho_{n}{\sim}\alpha\theta\log{1/\beta_{n}}
(see
(
2.4
)
),
